# Rodrigo Duterte

This article is about former Philippine president. For his grandson, see [Rigo Duterte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rigo_Duterte).

"DU30" and "Duterte" redirect here. For his presidential campaign stylized as "DU30", see [Rodrigo Duterte 2016 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rodrigo_Duterte_2016_presidential_campaign). For others with the surname, see [Duterte (surname)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duterte_\(surname\)).

In this [Filipino name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_name), the [middle name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_name) or maternal  [family name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surname) is *Roa* and the surname or paternal family name is *Duterte*.

| [v](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template\:Rodrigo_Duterte_sidebar)[t](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template_talk\:Rodrigo_Duterte_sidebar)[e](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Template\:Rodrigo_Duterte_sidebar) |

**Rodrigo Roa Duterte**[^cite_note-17][^cite_note-18] [KGCR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knights_of_Rizal) (born March 28, 1945) is a Filipino former politician who served as the 16th [president of the Philippines](271126.md) from 2016 to 2022.[^cite_note-19] He served as [mayor of Davao City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Davao_City) for three non-consecutive terms between 1988 and 2016.[^cite_note-note-1] Duterte is [the first Philippine president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_presidents_of_the_Philippines_by_province) from [Mindanao](20546.md),[^cite_note-20][^cite_note-21] and the oldest person to assume office, beginning his term at age 71.[^cite_note-22] Duterte is the chairman of [PDP–Laban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PDP%E2%80%93Laban), the ruling party during his presidency.

Born in [Maasin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maasin), Leyte (now in [Southern Leyte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Leyte)), Duterte moved to [Davao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davao_\(province\)) as a child where his father, [Vicente Duterte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicente_Duterte), served as provincial governor. He studied [political science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_science) at the [Lyceum of the Philippines University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyceum_of_the_Philippines_University), graduating in 1968, before obtaining a law degree from [San Beda College of Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Beda_College_of_Law) in 1972. He then worked as a lawyer and prosecutor for [Davao City](489401.md), before becoming vice mayor and, subsequently, mayor of the city in the wake of the 1986 [People Power Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People_Power_Revolution). Duterte won seven terms and served as mayor of Davao for over 22 years, during which the once crime-ridden city became peaceful and investor-friendly.[^cite_note-Anadolu-Ramos-2016-23][^cite_note-Dacumos-Lagorza-2017-24]

Duterte's [2016 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rodrigo_Duterte_2016_presidential_campaign) led to his [election victory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Philippine_presidential_election). During [his presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Rodrigo_Duterte), his domestic policy focused on combating the [illegal drug trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illegal_drug_trade_in_the_Philippines) by initiating the controversial [war on drugs](51032591.md), fighting crime and [corruption](28460051.md),[^cite_note-ArabNews-DuterteEyes-25][^cite_note-PNAGovPH-PACC-26][^cite_note-CNNPH-OfferedToResign-27] and intensified efforts against [terrorism](9101671.md) and [communist insurgency](18748169.md). He launched a [massive infrastructure plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Build!_Build!_Build!),[^cite_note-GulfNews-85BillionInfra-28][^cite_note-CNNPH-InfrastructureCOVID-29] initiated liberal economic reforms,[^cite_note-30] streamlined government processes,[^cite_note-31] and proposed a shift to a [federal system of government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalism_and_Rodrigo_Duterte) which was ultimately unsuccessful.[^cite_note-GMANews-FederalismCongress-32] He declared the intention to pursue an "independent foreign policy", and strengthened [relations with China](33734272.md) and [Russia](21761997.md)[^cite_note-independent_foreign_policy2-33] and also oversaw the controversial [burial of Ferdinand Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burial_of_Ferdinand_Marcos),[^cite_note-34][^cite_note-35] the [siege of Marawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Marawi),[^cite_note-36] and the [government's response](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_government_response_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic) to the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_the_Philippines).[^cite_note-37] Duterte praised the Armed Forces of the Philippines for their role in national security and disaster response, particularly highlighting their efforts during the siege of Marawi and in providing humanitarian assistance during natural calamities.[^cite_note-38]

Duterte's popularity and domestic approval rating remained relatively high throughout his presidency, and by the end of his term he was the most popular president since the 1986 revolution.[^cite_note-ABCNews-WeedonAlan-39][^cite_note-Philippine_News_Agency-40][^cite_note-Cruz-2022-41] He initially announced his candidacy for [vice president](2271639.md) in the [2022 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Philippine_presidential_election); in October 2021, he said he was retiring from politics. The next month, he filed his candidacy for senator but withdrew it on December 14.[^cite_note-PNAGovPH-SenateWidthdraw-42]

Duterte's [political positions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_positions_of_Rodrigo_Duterte) have been described as [populist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populism) and [nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_nationalism).[^cite_note-49] Duterte became known for his political ideology of [Dutertismo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutertismo). His political success has been aided by his vocal support for the [extrajudicial killing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extrajudicial_killings_and_forced_disappearances_in_the_Philippines) of drug users and criminals.[^cite_note-Reuters-DeathSquads-50] Duterte is allegedly the founder and head of the [Davao Death Squad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davao_Death_Squad), a vigilante squad of gangsters and former police that [summarily executed](1227309.md) more than 1,400 people by 2016.[^cite_note-\:1-51][^cite_note-52] Duterte has admitted to personally killing suspected criminals,[^cite_note-1988kidnap2-53][^cite_note-cannotlie2-54] and the usage of death squad.[^cite_note-55] His career has sparked [numerous protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protests_against_Rodrigo_Duterte) and attracted controversy, particularly over [human rights issues](4513322.md) and his controversial comments. The [International Criminal Court](14880.md) (ICC) opened a [preliminary investigation](68742643.md) into Duterte's drug war in 2018,[^cite_note-56] prompting Duterte to withdraw the Philippines from that body in response.[^cite_note-Yap-2020-57]

[Duterte was arrested](79430656.md) on March 11, 2025 and transferred to ICC custody in [The Hague](30269.md) the next day, making him the first Philippine president to face an international tribunal and the first Asian leader to face a trial before the ICC.[^cite_note-\:0-58][^cite_note-59] Despite this, he was re-elected as the mayor of Davao City in 2025; as he remains in The Hague, his son [Baste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sebastian_Duterte) is serving in his place as the acting mayor. As he failed to take his oath within the prescribed six-month period, he was disqualified from assuming office under the provisions of the Local Government Code. Duterte is scheduled to face trial for [crimes against humanity](61099.md) on November 30, 2026.[^cite_note-\:1-51]

## Early years

Rodrigo Roa Duterte was born on March 28, 1945, in [Maasin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maasin), Leyte.[^cite_note-returnshome-60] His father was [Vicente Gonzales Duterte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicente_Duterte) (1911–1968), a [Cebuano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cebuano_people) lawyer, and his mother, [Soledad Gonzales Roa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soledad_Roa-Duterte) (1916–2012), was a schoolteacher from [Cabadbaran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabadbaran), Agusan and a civic leader, of Chinese and Spanish mestizo descent.[^cite_note-Halili,_M.C._2010-61] Duterte has said that his grandfather was Chinese and hailed from [Xiamen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xiamen) in [Fujian](88585.md), China.[^cite_note-62] Duterte has four siblings: Eleanor (born 1941/42), Emmanuel, Jocelyn (1948–2023) and Benjamin ("Bong", 1953–2026).[^cite_note-63][^cite_note-64][^cite_note-65][^cite_note-66]

Duterte's father was mayor of [Danao, Cebu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danao,_Cebu), and subsequently the provincial governor of (the then-undivided) [Davao province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davao_\(province\)). Rodrigo's cousin [Ronald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ronald_Duterte) was mayor of [Cebu City](285868.md) from 1983 to 1986. Ronald's father, Ramon Duterte, also held that position from 1957 to 1959. The Dutertes consider the Cebu-based [political families](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_political_families_in_the_Philippines) of the Durano and the Almendras clan as relatives.[^cite_note-67][^cite_note-68] Duterte also has relatives from the Roa clan in Leyte through his mother's side.[^cite_note-DuterteZamboTimes-69] Duterte's family lived in Maasin, and in his father's hometown in Danao, until he was four years old.[^cite_note-PresidentGovPH-70] Duterte was heavily influenced by his mother, who unlike Vicente was a staunch anti-Marcos activist, which led the young Duterte to have a divided opinion on the Marcoses. Duterte later said Marcos could have been the best president, but he was a dictator.[^cite_note-71] The Dutertes initially moved to [Mindanao](20546.md) in 1948 but still went back and forth to the Visayas until 1949.[^cite_note-72] They finally settled in the [Davao Region](296162.md) in 1950. Vicente worked as a lawyer engaged in private practice. Soledad worked as a teacher until 1952, when Vicente entered politics.[^cite_note-RAFIOrgPH-SoledadRoa-73]

### Education and early law career

Duterte went to Laboon Elementary School in Maasin for a year.[^cite_note-DuterteZamboTimes-69] He spent his remaining elementary days at Santa Ana Elementary School in [Davao City](489401.md), where he completed his primary education in 1956. He finished his secondary education in the High School Department of Holy Cross College of Digos (now [Cor Jesu College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cor_Jesu_College)) in [Digos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digos), Davao province, after being expelled twice from previous schools, including one in the [Ateneo de Davao University (AdDU) High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ateneo_de_Davao_University#Senior_High_School) due to misconduct.[^cite_note-74] He graduated in 1968 with a [Bachelor of Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bachelor_of_Arts) degree in [political science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_science) at the [Lyceum of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyceum_of_the_Philippines_University) in [Manila](184334.md).[^cite_note-75][^cite_note-LPUDavao-Duterte-76]

Duterte obtained a law degree from [San Beda College of Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Beda_College_of_Law) in 1972,[^cite_note-77] passing the bar exam in the same year.[^cite_note-78] He worked as a professor in the national police academy, and was a member of an organization called Nationalist Alliance for Justice, Freedom and Democracy. He later served as the OIC vice mayor of Davao City, assisting evacuees from Davao City remote areas and working for the release of soldiers captured by the [New Peoples Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Peoples_Army) (NPA).[^cite_note-Gonzales-2005-79] Eventually, he became a special counsel at the City Prosecution Office in Davao City from 1977 to 1979, fourth assistant city prosecutor from 1979 to 1981, third assistant city prosecutor from 1981 to 1983, and second assistant city prosecutor from 1983 to 1986.[^cite_note-80][^cite_note-RedAndWhiteBall-81]

### Sexual abuse claims

Duterte has claimed he was [sexually abused by a priest](158934.md) when he was a minor.[^cite_note-Inquirer-SexuallyAbused-82] After he was challenged by the [Catholic Bishops' Conference of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Bishops'_Conference_of_the_Philippines) (CBCP) and AdDU officials to name the priest and file a case against him, Duterte then revealed the priest's name as Mark Falvey (died 1975).[^cite_note-Inquirer-SexuallyAbused-82] The [Jesuits of the Society of Jesus in the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Society_of_Jesus) confirmed that according to press reports in the United States, in May 2007, the Society of Jesus agreed to a tentative payout of US\$16 million to settle claims that Falvey sexually abused at least nine children in Los Angeles from 1959 to 1975. Accusations against Falvey began in 2002, although he was never charged with a crime. In May 2008, the [Diocese of Sacramento](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Sacramento) paid a \$100,000-settlement to a person allegedly raped and molested by Mark's brother, Arthur Falvey. However, it was not clearly indicated in the report if Mark Falvey was assigned at the Jesuit-run Ateneo de Davao.[^cite_note-83] When asked why he did not complain when the abuse supposedly happened, Duterte claimed that he was too young to complain about the priest's abuse and was intimidated by authorities at that time. He also stated that he never disclosed the abuse even after he was expelled and moved to a different high school, not even to his family.[^cite_note-GMA-TooYoung-84]

### Shooting of student at law school

Duterte stated at a rally in April 2016 that he shot a fellow student who had bullied him about his Visayan origin as well as other students of the same ethnicity, while at San Beda law college. He said, "But the truth is, I'm used to shooting people. When we were about to graduate from San Beda, I shot a person." Duterte said that he shot the student in a corridor at the college when the said student called him names again. He later told a reporter that the student survived, but refused to answer any further questions about the incident.[^cite_note-85]

However, in an interview aired on *[24 Oras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Mindanao)* and published on the official GMA News Online website on April 22, 2016, retired labor arbiter Arthur Amansec said Duterte and Octavio Goco at that time were both playing with a gun as it was normal for students to bring guns to school in the seventies. Amansec is Duterte's former classmate in San Beda College who witnessed the incident. He added that "the bullet hit the school's wooden floor and was embedded there." Amansec emphasized that Duterte and Goco remained friends until Goco died in the United States years later.[^cite_note-86]

## Political career in Davao City

See also: [1989 Davao hostage crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_Davao_hostage_crisis)



Mayor Duterte (left) with President [Benigno Aquino III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benigno_Aquino_III) during a meeting with local government unit leaders in Davao City in 2013

After the 1986 [People Power Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People_Power_Revolution) that toppled the regime of President [Ferdinand Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_Marcos), Duterte was appointed officer-in-charge vice mayor of Davao City by President [Corazon Aquino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corazon_Aquino).[^cite_note-87] In the [1988 local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Davao_City_elections), he ran for [mayor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Davao_City) under Lakas ng Dabaw,[^cite_note-88] a local political alliance, defeating former OIC Mayor [Zafiro Respicio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zafiro_Respicio) by a narrow margin of 6,000 votes.[^cite_note-89]

As mayor of Davao City, Duterte made efforts to unite the different tribes and political groups in the city. He set a precedent by designating deputy mayors to represent the administrative districts, as well as the [Lumad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lumad_peoples) and [Moro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moro_people) peoples in the city government; this was later copied by other cities in other parts of the Philippines.[^cite_note-Gonzales-2005-79]

In December 1990, Duterte joined the [Nacionalista Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nacionalista_Party) upon the persuasion of Senator [Juan Ponce Enrile](3322486.md).[^cite_note-MSBigornia-6][^cite_note-90] In [1992](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Davao_City_local_elections), he successfully defended his seat from [1st district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davao_City's_1st_congressional_district) representative [Prospero Nograles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prospero_Nograles). In 1995, after [Flor Contemplacion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flor_Contemplacion), a Filipina, was executed in [Singapore](27318.md) after confessing to a double murder, Duterte allegedly burned a [flag of Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Singapore) (though this claim was later denied) and joined 1,000 employees of Davao City in protest.[^cite_note-91][^cite_note-92]

In 1998, because he was term-limited to run again for mayor, Duterte ran and won as congressman of the city's [1st district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davao_City's_1st_congressional_district) under [Laban ng Makabayang Masang Pilipino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laban_ng_Makabayang_Masang_Pilipino). He was a member of five House committees, namely: [National Defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_House_Committee_on_National_Defense_and_Security), [Public Order and Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_House_Committee_on_Public_Order_and_Safety), [Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_House_Committee_on_Health), [Transportation and Communications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_House_Committee_on_Transportation), and [Cooperative Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_House_Committee_on_Cooperatives_Development). He filed 64 measures, including 45 bills, with one enacted into law—Republic Act No. 8969, which declared the third Friday of August a special holiday in Davao City. He expressed disinterest with his new post, describing it as less engaging compared to his time as mayor. He attempted to resign in 1999 after his son [Paolo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paolo_Duterte) was accused of mauling a hotel guard, but House Speaker [Manny Villar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manny_Villar) and President [Joseph Estrada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Estrada) declined his resignation.[^cite_note-93]

After embarking on a diplomatic trip to [Brazil](3383.md) between 2000 and 2001, Duterte made a brief stopover in the [United States](3434750.md), but as he was about to step out of the [Los Angeles International Airport](18131.md), he was held up by law enforcement officers and interrogated about his travel documents. After the stopover, Duterte never again returned to the United States.[^cite_note-UR-94][^cite_note-95]

In 2001, he was elected mayor of Davao for a fourth unconsecutive term, defeating his former ally, incumbent Mayor Benjamin de Guzman; his estranged sister Jocelyn attempted to run against him as well, but was disqualified by the [Commission on Elections](1847922.md).[^cite_note-96][^cite_note-97] He was re-elected in 2004, defeating de Guzman for the second time,[^cite_note-98] and in [2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Davao_City_local_elections) with virtually no opposition.[^cite_note-99] In 2003, Duterte formally laid out the scope of Davao City's Chinatown, the first Chinatown in Mindanao, along the newly renamed Magsaysay Street, while establishing the Davao City Chinatown Development Council to oversee the area's development.[^cite_note-UR-94][^cite_note-100]



Newly elected Davao City Vice Mayor Duterte reading his inaugural speech in June 2010

In 2010, he was elected vice mayor, succeeding his daughter [Sara](42504861.md), who was elected as mayor.[^cite_note-101]



Mayor Duterte (left) and Mayor [Edgardo Pamintuan Sr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edgardo_Pamintuan_Sr.) (right) signing an agreement in 2015

Duterte was elected unopposed for his seventh term in 2013. In November of that year, Davao City sent rescue and medical teams to [Tacloban](746499.md) to give aid to the victims of [Typhoon Haiyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Typhoon_Haiyan) (locally known in the country as Typhoon Yolanda). Financial assistance was also given to [Bohol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bohol) and [Cebu](160278.md) for [earthquake victims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Bohol_earthquake).[^cite_note-SunStar-2013QuakeAid-102]

Duterte also passed Davao City's Women Development Code, which aims "to uphold the rights of women and the belief in their worth and dignity as human beings".[^cite_note-DavaoCityGovPH-GADAwards-103][^cite_note-104] Duterte banned swimsuit competitions in beauty pageants in Davao City.[^cite_note-GMANews-SwimsuitBan-105] He gained prominence for supporting the first-ever [Gawad Kalinga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gawad_Kalinga) Village inside a jail facility in Davao City. It is a home-type jail with ten cottages built inside the compound, which now serve as homes for female inmates.[^cite_note-PhilStar-GawadKalinga-106]

### Law and order



Duterte speaks with Davao City residents in 2009.

During Duterte's tenure as mayor, [Davao City](489401.md) experienced economic boom and a significant decrease in crime from being a conflict-ridden area between communists and right-wing groups during the 1970s and 1980s, and is constantly rated as among the safest in the country.[^cite_note-Heydarian-2017-107][^cite_note-Dacumos-Lagorza-2017-24] The city also ranks high in the world according to crowdsourced survey site [Numbeo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Numbeo),[^cite_note-108][^cite_note-109] a narrative that gained currency in the national media, creating a widespread public perception that has been a significant factor in establishing support for his [nationwide drug policy](51032591.md).[^cite_note-110][^cite_note-111][^cite_note-112] The city was also awarded "Most Child-Friendly City for Highly-Urbanized Category" in 1998, 1999, 2013 and 2014.[^cite_note-113][^cite_note-114]

Under Duterte's watch, the city council imposed a prohibition on selling, serving, drinking, and consuming alcoholic beverages from 01\:00 until 08\:00 each morning. Duterte signed Executive Order No. 39, reducing the speed limits for all kinds of motor vehicles within the territorial jurisdiction of Davao City in the interest of public safety and order.[^cite_note-115] Duterte also signed Executive Order No. 04 creating the implementing of rules and regulations for a new comprehensive anti-smoking ordinance.[^cite_note-116] A [firecracker ban](418188.md#Firecracker_ban) was also implemented by the City Council through the support of Duterte.[^cite_note-cnnphdvo-117] Davao acquired 10 ambulances for [central 911](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9-1-1) intended for medical emergencies and 42 mobile patrol vehicles and motorcycles for the Davao City Police Office.[^cite_note-118] Duterte, through Executive Order No. 24, ordered all shopping malls and commercial centers to install, operate and maintain high end and high definition closed circuit television (CCTV) cameras at all entrance and exit points of their premises.[^cite_note-119]

In September 2015, Duterte was contacted by a restaurant owner in [Davao City](489401.md) after a local tourist refused to comply with the city's public anti-smoking ordinance. The then-mayor went to the restaurant and had the tourist choose to be shot at his crotch, have himself imprisoned, or swallow his cigarette butt; the tourist chose the latter and apologized to Duterte.[^cite_note-Inquirer-CigaretteSwallow-120] Duterte was then met with criticisms especially from the [Commission on Human Rights (CHR)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commission_on_Human_Rights_\(Philippines\)).[^cite_note-121]

### Alleged involvement with Davao Death Squad

Main article: [Davao Death Squad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davao_Death_Squad)

Further information: [Philippine drug war § Senate committee](51032591.md#Senate_committee), and [Philippine drug war § Arturo Lascañas](51032591.md#Arturo_Lasca%C3%B1as)

Duterte has been linked by human rights groups such as [Amnesty International](18947898.md) and [Human Rights Watch](46530.md) to [extrajudicial killings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extrajudicial_killing) of over 1,400 alleged criminals and [street children](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Street_children_in_the_Philippines) by [vigilante](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vigilante) [death squads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_squad).[^cite_note-HRWOrg-TheRise-122] In the April 2009 UN General Assembly of the Human Rights Council, the UN report (Eleventh Session Agenda item 3, par 21) said, "The Mayor of Davao City has done nothing to prevent these killings, and his public comments suggest that he is, in fact, supportive."\[^cite_note-[www.ohchr.org-123](http://www.ohchr.org-123/)] Duterte emphasized that the concept of human rights for criminals is Western and should not apply to the Philippines;[^cite_note-Inquirer-Lacorte-2015-124] he further challenged human rights officials to file a case against him if they could provide evidence of his links with vigilante groups.[^cite_note-125]

> We're the ninth-safest city. How do you think I did it? How did I reach that title among the world's safest cities? Kill them all \[criminals].

— Duterte, May 15, 2015[^cite_note-Inquirer-Lacorte-2015-124]

Duterte frequently expressed his support for the extrajudicial killings that occurred during his tenure as Davao City mayor, while alternately denying and admitting his links to them.[^cite_note-Human_Rights_Watch-126]\[^cite_note-[www.news.com.au-127](http://www.news.com.au-127/)] During his post-presidency in October 2023, he admitted on his talk show program *[Gikan sa Masa, para sa Masa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gikan_sa_Masa,_para_sa_Masa)* to using intelligence funds to finance the extrajudicial killings done during his time as mayor.[^cite_note-128][^cite_note-129]

### Federalism advocacy

Main article: [Federalism and Rodrigo Duterte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalism_and_Rodrigo_Duterte)

In September 2014, Duterte and former mayors and governors, calling themselves the Mindanao Council of Leaders, advocated for a federalist government.[^cite_note-130] A month later, Duterte attended an event sponsored by the Federal Movement for a Better Philippines in [Cebu City](285868.md).[^cite_note-131]

In December 2014, Duterte held a summit entitled "Mindanaons Forging Unity Toward a Federal System of Government".[^cite_note-132]

## 2016 presidential campaign

Main article: [Rodrigo Duterte 2016 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rodrigo_Duterte_2016_presidential_campaign)

Further information: [2016 Philippine presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Philippine_presidential_election)



Duterte–Cayetano 2016 campaign logo

Prospects of Duterte running for president in the 2016 elections began in February 2015, during which Duterte, then outgoing [Mayor of Davao City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Davao_City), re-entered the [PDP-Laban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PDP-Laban) party, who courted him to run for president.[^cite_note-133] A month later, Duterte emerged as a preferred presidential candidate in a [Pulse Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulse_Asia) poll; tied with Manila mayor and former president [Joseph Estrada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Estrada), Duterte ranked third behind Senator [Grace Poe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grace_Poe) and frontrunner, Vice President [Jejomar Binay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jejomar_Binay).[^cite_note-134] In the following months leading to October, however, Duterte repeatedly declined his supporters' clamors for him to run as president because of what he described as a "flawed government system", as well as lack of funds and opposition from his family members—notably, from his daughter [Sara](42504861.md).[^cite_note-135][^cite_note-Ranada-2015-136] Additionally, Duterte said he will retire from politics after his mayorship ends, but only if Sara runs as mayor.[^cite_note-Ranada-2015-136]

PDP-Laban [standard bearer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_bearer) [Martin Diño](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Di%C3%B1o) unexpectedly withdrew his candidacy on October 29 and named Duterte as his substitute.[^cite_note-137] A month later, Duterte formally announced his presidential bid and accepted [Alan Peter Cayetano](10220741.md)'s offer to be his running mate; Duterte also named Sara as his substitute for Mayor of Davao City.[^cite_note-138][^cite_note-139]



Duterte (3rd from right) and allies campaigning in [Pandacan, Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pandacan,_Manila) on April 23, 2016

During his campaign, Duterte gained distinction for his emphatic anti-crime message and push to reduce "[Imperial Manila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Manila)'s" dominance.[^cite_note-Cook-Salazar-2016-140]<sup>: 5 </sup> At the same time, he made controversial remarks, including promising to kill tens of thousands of criminals and eradicate crime in six months.[^cite_note-TheGuardian-DeclaresVictory-141][^cite_note-ABC6-142] He also said his presidency would be "a bloody one", and that he would pardon himself, the police, and soldiers accused of human rights abuses at the end of his six-year term.[^cite_note-ABC6-142] Duterte also apologized after saying out of anger when recalling the events[^cite_note-143] that he "should have been first" to rape Jacqueline Hamill, an Australian missionary who was [gang-raped](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gang-rape) and killed during the [1989 Davao hostage crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_Davao_hostage_crisis).[^cite_note-144] After the United States' and Australia's ambassadors to the Philippines criticized him for the rape comments, Duterte threatened to sever diplomatic ties with the countries if elected.[^cite_note-ABC6-142]

### Constitutional reforms

Main article: [Federalism and Rodrigo Duterte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalism_and_Rodrigo_Duterte)

Duterte campaigned for [decentralization](49139.md) and a shift to a federal government during the 2016 presidential election.[^cite_note-TheGuardian-DeclaresVictory-141] In an October 2014 forum organized by *Federal Movement for a Better Philippines* in [Cebu City](285868.md) prior to joining the presidential race, the then-mayor of Davao City called for the creation of two federal states for [Moro people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moro_people) as a solution to the problems besetting [Mindanao](20546.md).[^cite_note-inq-145] Mayor Duterte said that [Nur Misuari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nur_Misuari) and his [Moro National Liberation Front](2821886.md) do not see eye-to-eye with the [Moro Islamic Liberation Front](51673.md) which the administration of President [Benigno Aquino III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benigno_Aquino_III) had inked a peace deal with. He also said that the "template of the [Bangsamoro Basic Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangsamoro_Basic_Law) is federal", but what is granted to the Bangsamoro should also be granted to other Moro groups and other regions in the country.[^cite_note-chacha-146] In a dialogue with the [Makati Business Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makati_Business_Club) prior to the elections, Duterte said he is open to "toning down the Constitution" to accommodate more foreign investors to the Philippines.[^cite_note-mb-147] He also said he is open to up to 70 percent foreign ownership of businesses in the country and foreign lease of lands up to 60 years, but will "leave it to Congress to decide".[^cite_note-mb-147]

### Election to the presidency

On May 30, 2016, the [16th Congress of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/16th_Congress_of_the_Philippines) proclaimed Duterte as the [president-elect of the Philippines](271126.md) after he topped the [official count](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_canvass_for_the_Philippine_presidential_election,_2016) by the [Congress of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_the_Philippines) for the [2016 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Philippine_presidential_election) with 16,601,997 votes, 6.6 million more than his closest rival, [Mar Roxas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mar_Roxas).[^cite_note-148][^cite_note-149][^cite_note-150] [Camarines Sur](255883.md) representative [Leni Robredo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leni_Robredo) on the other hand, was proclaimed as the [vice president-elect of the Philippines](2271639.md) with 14,418,817 votes, narrowly defeating Senator [Bongbong Marcos](572460.md) by 263,473 votes.[^cite_note-Inquirer_1-151]

Duterte's election victory was attributed to growing public dissatisfaction on the perceived failures of administrations that followed the [1986 EDSA Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_EDSA_Revolution).[^cite_note-152] It was also aided by a strong social media and grassroots fanbase[^cite_note-153][^cite_note-Cook-Salazar-2016-140]<sup>: 8–10 </sup> and the endorsement of the influential [Iglesia ni Cristo](288343.md), known for its [block-voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Block-voting).[^cite_note-154]

## Presidency (2016–2022)

Main article: [Presidency of Rodrigo Duterte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Rodrigo_Duterte)

See also: [Presidential transition of Rodrigo Duterte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_transition_of_Rodrigo_Duterte) and [Inauguration of Rodrigo Duterte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inauguration_of_Rodrigo_Duterte)

| Presidential styles of **Rodrigo Duterte[^cite_note-CNNPhilippines-HisExcellency-155][^cite_note-Inquirer-HisExcellency-156]** |  |
| --- | --- |
|  |  |
| [Reference style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Style_\(manner_of_address\)) | President Duterte, [His Excellency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/His_Excellency) (rarely used)[^cite_note-his-excellency-157][^cite_note-Inquirer-HisExcellency-156] |
| Spoken style | Your Excellency (rarely used)[^cite_note-his-excellency-157] |
| Alternative style | Mr. President, President, Mayor[^cite_note-158] |

Duterte was inaugurated as the [sixteenth president of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Philippine_Presidents#Presidents) on June 30, 2016, succeeding [Benigno Aquino III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benigno_Aquino_III). At age 71, Duterte became the oldest person elected to the [presidency](271126.md), the first local chief executive to get elected straight to the Office of the President, the second [Cebuano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cebuano_people) president (after [Sergio Osmeña](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergio_Osme%C3%B1a)), and the first [Visayan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visayans) president from [Mindanao](20546.md).[^cite_note-rodylenibynumbers2-159]

In 2019, he became the first Philippine president since 1986 not to publicize his [statement of assets, liabilities, and net worth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statement_of_assets,_liabilities,_and_net_worth) (SALN);[^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161] his SALNs since 2019 would only be revealed post-presidency in November 2025.[^cite_note-162][^cite_note-163]

### Early actions

Further information: [First 100 days of Rodrigo Duterte's presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_100_days_of_Rodrigo_Duterte's_presidency)



President-elect Duterte (left) and outgoing President [Benigno Aquino III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benigno_Aquino_III) at [Malacañang Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaca%C3%B1ang_Palace) on inauguration day, June 30, 2016

Shortly after his inauguration, Duterte held his first Cabinet meeting to lay out their first agenda, which included the country's [disaster risk reduction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disaster_risk_reduction) management, decongesting [Ninoy Aquino International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ninoy_Aquino_International_Airport) in Manila; he also expressed his ideas and concerns regarding the [territorial disputes in the South China Sea](32005536.md) prior to the announcement of the verdict of the [Philippines' arbitration case against China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines_v._China) over the issue,[^cite_note-164] which the Philippines later won.[^cite_note-nyt4-165] On July 4, he issued his first [executive order](210541.md), allowing his [Cabinet Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_Secretary_\(Philippines\)) to supervise over several agencies that focus on [poverty reduction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_reduction).[^cite_note-166] He called for the reimposition of [capital punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_punishment_in_the_Philippines) in the country to execute criminals involved in "heinous" crimes, such as [illegal drug trade](218449.md), insisting on [hanging](155019.md).[^cite_note-167]

During his first 100 days in office, Duterte issued an [executive order on freedom of information](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_Information_Order_\(Philippines\)),[^cite_note-168] launched an intensified [campaign against illegal drugs](51032591.md), sought to resume peace talks with [communist insurgents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_rebellion_in_the_Philippines), formulated a comprehensive tax reform plan, led efforts to pass the [Bangsamoro Basic Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangsamoro_Basic_Law), made efforts to streamline government transactions, launched the nationwide [9–1–1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9-1-1_\(Philippines\)) rescue and [8888](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/8888_\(Philippines\)) complaint hotlines, established a one-stop service center for [overseas Filipino workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overseas_Filipino_workers), and increased in the combat and incentive pay of soldiers and police personnel.[^cite_note-ManilaTimes-First100Days-169]

Duterte made moves to limit [US visiting troops](10392416.md) in the country, and has reached out to China and Russia to improve relations. He launched tirades against international critics, particularly, United States President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama), the US government, the [United Nations](31769.md), and the [European Union](9317.md), which expressed condemnation to his unprecedented war on drugs that led to the deaths of about 3,300 people, half of which were killed by unknown assailants, and the arrest of 22,000 drug suspects and surrender of about 731,000 people.[^cite_note-ManilaTimes-First100Days-169][^cite_note-Inquirer-1st100Days-170]

Duterte takes his [oath of office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_presidential_inauguration#Oath_of_Office) as the 16th president of the Philippines on June 30, 2016.

Following the [September 2 bombing in Davao City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Davao_City_bombing) that killed 14 people in the city's central business district, on September 3 Duterte declared a "state of lawlessness", and on the following day issued [Proclamation No. 55](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation_No._55) to officially declare a "[state of national emergency](298608.md) on account of lawless violence in Mindanao".[^cite_note-time-171][^cite_note-philstar-172] On December 7, Duterte signed [Executive Order No. 10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Executive_Order_10) creating a consultative committee to review the 1987 [Constitution of the Philippines](19554336.md).[^cite_note-gma2-173]

Duterte adjusted to working and residing at the [Malacañang Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaca%C3%B1ang_Palace) by dividing his workweek between Manila and Davao City, spending three days in each city and utilizing the [Malacañang of the South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaca%C3%B1ang_of_the_South) while in Davao.[^cite_note-174]

A [Pulse Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulse_Asia) survey conducted from July 2–8 showed that Duterte had a trust rating of 91%, the highest of the six presidents since the [Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_Marcos) dictatorship.[^cite_note-dip2-175] In December 2016, Duterte was ranked 70th on *[Forbes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes)* [list of The World's Most Powerful People](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes_list_of_The_World's_Most_Powerful_People).[^cite_note-176][^cite_note-177]

### Domestic policy

#### Economic policy

Further information: [DuterteNomics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DuterteNomics)

Duterte's socioeconomic policies, referred to as *DuterteNomics*, include tax reform, infrastructure development, social protection programs, and other policies to promote economic growth and human development in the country.[^cite_note-timeline-178][^cite_note-179] [Finance Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Finance_\(Philippines\)) [Carlos Dominguez III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Dominguez_III) has said that the government required what he describes as an "audacious" economic strategy in order for the Philippines to "catch up with its more vibrant neighbors" by 2022 and help it achieve [high-income economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-income_economy) status within a generation. The term *DuterteNomics* was coined to describe the economic policy of the Duterte administration.

Duterte initiated liberal economic reforms to attract foreign investors.[^cite_note-180] In March 2022, he signed Republic Act No. 11647 which amended the Foreign Investment Act of 1991, effectively relaxing restrictions on foreign investments by allowing foreigners to invest in a local enterprise up to 100% of its capital.[^cite_note-181] He signed Republic Act No. 11659, amending the 85-year-old Public Service Act, allowing full foreign ownership of public services, which include airports, expressways, railways, telecommunications, and shipping industries, in the country.[^cite_note-182]

Duterte reformed the country's tax system in an effort to make the country's tax system fairer, simpler, and more efficient.[^cite_note-TaxBits-2022-183] In December 2017, he signed [Tax Reform for Acceleration and Inclusion Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_Reform_for_Acceleration_and_Inclusion_Law) (TRAIN Law) which excludes those earning an annual taxable income of ₱250,000 and below from paying the personal income tax, while raising higher [excise taxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Excise_tax) on vehicles, sugar-sweetened beverages, petroleum products, tobacco and other non-essential goods.[^cite_note-184] Revenues collected from the TRAIN law will help fund the administration's massive [infrastructure program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Build!_Build!_Build!).[^cite_note-ABSCBN-TaxReform-185][^cite_note-ABS-CBN-TRAINLaw-186] In March 2021, to attract more investments and maintain fiscal stability, Duterte signed the Corporate Recovery and Tax Incentives for Enterprises (CREATE) Act, reducing the 30 percent [corporate income tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corporate_income_tax) rate to 25 percent for firms with assets above [₱](227672.md)100 million and to 20 percent for smaller firms.[^cite_note-187] Duterte raised [sin taxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sin_tax) on tobacco and vapor products in July 2019, and alcohol beverages and [electronic cigarettes](11996885.md) in January 2020, to fund the Universal Health Care Act and reduce incidence of deaths and diseases associated with smoking and alcohol consumption.[^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189]

Duterte's tax reform program has garnered both support and criticism. Critics have argued that the administration's tax policy would burden the poor.[^cite_note-190] The implementation of the TRAIN Law triggered protests from various left-wing groups. On January 15, 2018, protesters gathered at various public market sites, calling for the revocation of TRAIN.[^cite_note-191] However, proponents of the program cite its appeal to foreign investors and economic benefits as the main reasons behind tax reform.

#### Infrastructure development

Main article: [Build! Build! Build!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Build!_Build!_Build!)



The [Athletics Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Clark_City_Athletics_Stadium) of the [New Clark City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Clark_City) sports complex, completed on October 12, 2019, 50 days before the opening of the [2019 Southeast Asian Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Southeast_Asian_Games)

[^cite_note-192]

To reduce poverty, encourage economic growth, and reduce congestion in [Metro Manila](72340.md), the Duterte administration launched its comprehensive infrastructure program, [Build, Build, Build](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Build,_Build,_Build),[^cite_note-pcoo-193] on April 18, 2017.[^cite_note-194] The program, which forms part of [the administration's socioeconomic policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DuterteNomics),[^cite_note-pcoo-193] aimed to usher in the country's "Golden Age of Infrastructure" by increasing the share of spending on public infrastructure in the country's [gross domestic product](12594.md) (GDP) from 5.4 percent in 2017 to 7.4 percent in 2022.[^cite_note-BusinessMirror-BBB-195][^cite_note-196] The administration, in 2017, shifted its infrastructure funding policy from [public-private partnerships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public-private_partnership) (PPPs) of previous administrations to government revenues and [official development assistance](1316305.md) (ODA), particularly from Japan and China,[^cite_note-197] but has since October 2019 engaged with the private sector for additional funding.[^cite_note-198][^cite_note-199]

The administration revised its list of Infrastructure Flagship Projects (IFPs) under the Build, Build, Build program from 75 to 100 in November 2019,[^cite_note-ABS-CBN-List100-200][^cite_note-ABS-CBN-List100PDF-201] then to 104, and finally, to 112 in 2020,[^cite_note-PhilStar-Inherit88Infra-202] expanding its scope to include health, information and communications technology, and water infrastructure projects to support the country's economic growth and recovery from the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_the_Philippines). Some major projects include[^cite_note-ABS-CBN-List100PDF-201] the Subic-Clark Railway,[^cite_note-Mawis-2018-203] the [North–South Commuter Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North%E2%80%93South_Commuter_Railway) from [New Clark City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Clark_City) to [Calamba, Laguna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calamba,_Laguna),[^cite_note-Mawis-2018-203] the [Metro Manila Subway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro_Manila_Subway_Line_9),[^cite_note-204] the expansion of [Clark International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clark_International_Airport),[^cite_note-Mawis-2018-203] the [Mindanao Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mindanao_Railway) (Tagum-Davao-Digos Segment),[^cite_note-205] and the [Luzon Spine Expressway Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luzon_Spine_Expressway_Network).[^cite_note-Lamentillo-2018-206][^cite_note-The_Summit_Express-2018-207] By April 2022, 12 IFPs have been completed by the administration, while 88 IFPs, which were on their "advanced stage", have been passed on to the succeeding administration for completion.[^cite_note-PhilStar-Inherit88Infra-202]

From June 2016 to July 2021, a total of 29,264 kilometers (18,184 mi) of roads, 5,950 bridges, 11,340 flood control projects, 222 evacuation centers, and 150,149 elementary and secondary classrooms, and 653 COVID-19 facilities under the Build, Build, Build program had been completed.[^cite_note-208][^cite_note-MB-Nov2019-209]

#### War on drugs

Main article: [Philippine drug war](51032591.md)



Duterte with Governor [Lilia Pineda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lilia_Pineda) inspects a seized [shabu](11038318.md) laboratory in [Arayat, Pampanga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arayat,_Pampanga) on September 27, 2016.

Duterte placed the [Philippines' illegal drugs problem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illegal_drug_trade_in_the_Philippines) at the forefront of his presidential campaign, warning the Philippines was at risk of becoming a [narco-state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narco-state).[^cite_note-Inquirer-DigongDefends-210] Upon entering office, Duterte began a nationwide crackdown against illegal drugs. At the forefront of the campaign's operations was *Oplan Tokhang*, which involved policemen knocking on the doors of suspected drug users and inviting them to surrender.[^cite_note-FilipinoTimes-PHNeedsMoreRahab-211] Some described the anti-drug campaign as a form of [genocide](12441.md) against drug users, in part due to Duterte's early comparison of himself to [Adolf Hitler](2731583.md).[^cite_note-212][^cite_note-213][^cite_note-214] Among Duterte's early actions included naming three Chinese nationals as alleged [drug lords](23002805.md) in the Philippines,[^cite_note-215] calling for assistance from the [New Peoples Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Peoples_Army) to join the crackdown,[^cite_note-216] and [publicly naming 150 public officials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rodrigo_Duterte's_August_7,_2016,_speech) allegedly involved in the drug trade.[^cite_note-217]

Concerns arose worldwide due to the rising death toll during police operations,[^cite_note-218] and drug suspects reportedly fighting back ("*nanlaban*") was a common alibi of several policemen involved.[^cite_note-219] At the height of the drug crackdown in his early presidency, Duterte urged the policemen to kill drug addicts,[^cite_note-220] promising to pardon them for performing their duties;[^cite_note-221][^cite_note-222] Duterte's call prompted thousands to surrender,[^cite_note-FilipinoTimes-PHNeedsMoreRahab-211][^cite_note-Inquirer-1st100Days-170] and observers noted this assurance may have emboldened the police to allegedly execute suspects and plant evidence on them.[^cite_note-223] In August 2016, opposition Senator [Leila de Lima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leila_de_Lima) launched a probe into [extrajudicial killings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extrajudicial_killings_in_the_Philippines), focusing on cases that took place during Duterte's tenure as mayor of Davao City. De Lima presented as witness an alleged member of Davao City-based vigilante group [Davao Death Squad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davao_Death_Squad), [Edgar Matobato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edgar_Matobato), who testified that Duterte was involved in extrajudicial killings in the city. Duterte dismissed the allegations as fabricated,[^cite_note-224] and the Senate committee formally terminated the inquiry on October 13, 2016, citing a lack of corroborating evidence.[^cite_note-225] Shortly after, Duterte implicated De Lima in the illegal drug trade during her tenure as Justice Secretary under the Aquino III administration. Though De Lima denied the claims, she was later imprisoned in February 2017 due to the [drug charges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Bilibid_Prison_drug_trafficking_scandal).[^cite_note-226] Two months later, Matobato's lawyer, Jude Josue Sabio, filed charges for [crimes against humanity](61099.md) with the [International Criminal Court](14880.md) against Duterte and 11 other officials.[^cite_note-227]

Responding to public clamor, in October 2017, Duterte designated the [Philippine Drug Enforcement Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Drug_Enforcement_Agency) to lead anti-drug operations and temporarily barred the police from joining the raids.[^cite_note-PhilStar-PDEASoleAgancy-228][^cite_note-PhilStar-PNPBack-229] Through an executive order he issued in October 2018, Duterte instituted a more balanced approach to the drug problem by establishing the Philippine Anti-Illegal Drugs Strategy, which ordered all government departments and [state universities and colleges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Universities_and_Colleges_\(Philippines\)) to implement their own strategies in tackling the country's illegal drug trade; the order also urged local government units, non-government organizations, and private institutions to support and implement the Strategy.[^cite_note-230]



Protesters against the [war on drugs](51032591.md) gather in front of the Philippine Consulate General in [New York City](645042.md) on October 11, 2016.

Duterte campaigned to eliminate illegal drugs in the country within three to six months, but later acknowledged he miscalculated the severity of the problem as he based his approach to that of [Davao City](489401.md) during his tenure as the city's mayor. Stating it would take him his entire term to eradicate it, he cited the difficulty in border control due to the country's fragmented geography and lamented that several government officials and law enforcers were involved in drug trafficking.[^cite_note-231] Before leaving office, he implored president-elect [Bongbong Marcos](572460.md) to continue tackling the illegal drug problem,[^cite_note-232] but declined an appointment offer as Marcos' [drug czar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drug_czar), expressing a desire to retire.[^cite_note-MB-DeclinesDrugCzar-233] By the end of Duterte's term, the number of drug suspects killed since Duterte took office was officially tallied by the government as 6,252;[^cite_note-234] human rights groups, however, claim drug casualties reached as high as 12,000 to 30,000.[^cite_note-235]

Various international publications and media companies had claimed that Duterte's anti-drugs campaign was a war against the poor due to the abject poverty of those arrested or killed.[^cite_note-236][^cite_note-237] On June 19, 2018, 38 [United Nations](31769.md) member states released a collective statement through the [United Nations Human Rights Council](635790.md), calling on the Philippines and Duterte to end the killings in the war on drugs and cooperate in investigating human rights abuses.[^cite_note-238][^cite_note-PhilStar-38StatesCall-239] Though many human rights note that Duterte's war on drugs was a stain on his legacy,[^cite_note-240] the anti-narcotics drive received domestic approval during his term,[^cite_note-241][^cite_note-Reuters-SWSPoll-242][^cite_note-243] and 58% of the country's barangays were declared by the government cleared of illegal drugs by February 2022.[^cite_note-244][^cite_note-PNAGovPH-24KVillages-245]

#### International Criminal Court

Further information: [International Criminal Court investigation in the Philippines](68742643.md)

In April 2017, a case was filed by lawyer Jude Sabio before the [International Criminal Court](14880.md) (ICC), charging Duterte and 11 other officials with [crimes against humanity](61099.md) in relation to the deaths of the controversial war on drugs; in response, outgoing ICC chief prosecutor [Fatou Bensouda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatou_Bensouda) in early 2018 launched a preliminary examination into the charges against the accused. This prompted Duterte in March 2018 to initiate the withdrawal of the Philippines from the ICC,[^cite_note-246] which took effect on March 17, 2019.[^cite_note-Cabico-Patag-2019-247] Duterte rebuked the idea of allowing foreigners to meddle in the country's justice system and his administration emphasized that cases against him should be filed before the national courts.[^cite_note-DW-2018-248][^cite_note-Cabico-Patag-2019-247] Additionally, he argued that the [Rome Statute](68762.md), which was ratified by the Senate in 2011, was never binding in the Philippines as it was never published in the [Official Gazette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_Gazette_\(Philippines\)), a requirement for a law in the country to take effect.[^cite_note-249] For its part, the ICC reiterated that it maintains jurisdiction over crimes which took place before the withdrawal.[^cite_note-Cabico-Patag-2019-247]

Stating that his 2017 case was an orchestrated move by the [Liberal Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_Party_\(Philippines\)), notably Senators [Antonio Trillanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_Trillanes) and [Leila de Lima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leila_de_Lima), to discredit Duterte, Sabio retracted his allegations in January 2020 and requested the ICC to dismiss the case.[^cite_note-250] The ICC rejected Sabio's request and asserted that it "cannot effectively destroy or return information once it is in its possession or control".[^cite_note-251] Meanwhile, Duterte's ICC withdrawal was challenged before the [Supreme Court](1335534.md) by three petitions filed by the Philippine Coalition for the ICC, the [Integrated Bar of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integrated_Bar_of_the_Philippines), and opposition senators; though the Court in March 2021 ultimately dismissed all three cases on the grounds that the petitioners were unable to establish legal standing to challenge the ICC withdrawal, the court ruled that the president has no "unbridled authority" to withdraw from treaties.[^cite_note-252]

The ICC, on September 16, 2021, eventually authorized a formal investigation into the war on drugs[^cite_note-253] in the Philippines, focusing on crimes committed between 2016 and March 2019.[^cite_note-254] In response, the Philippine government in November 2021 requested a deferral of the probe, which was suspended by the ICC to assess the request.[^cite_note-255] Four days before Duterte left office, however, ICC prosecutor [Karim Ahmad Khan](11606957.md) requested the pre-trial chamber of the ICC to immediately resume the investigation after concluding that the deferral request was "not warranted".[^cite_note-256] The succeeding Marcos administration initially declined to allow any ICC probe in the country due to sovereignty issues;[^cite_note-257] however, due to tensions between the Duterte and Marcos political families, the Marcos administration is seen as increasingly open to cooperating with the ICC.[^cite_note-Politiko-IsangSalita-258][^cite_note-Bolledo-2023-259] Duterte stated he remained unfazed at the threat of arrest, saying he would rather "die first" before facing a foreign court. [Salvador Panelo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvador_Panelo), who served as Duterte's chief legal counsel, alleged that Duterte's political opponents were using the ICC as a "political and propaganda apparatus".[^cite_note-260]

#### Mindanao insurgency

Further information: [Bangsamoro peace process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangsamoro_peace_process) and [Moro conflict](4602307.md)



Duterte (center) with other officials during the presentation of the [Bangsamoro Organic Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangsamoro_Organic_Law) to the [MILF](51673.md) at [Malacañang Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaca%C3%B1ang_Palace) on August 6, 2018

Duterte has said that Moro dignity is what the MILF and MNLF are struggling for, and that they are not terrorists. He acknowledged that the Moros were subjected to wrongdoing, historical and in territory.[^cite_note-261]

Duterte was endorsed in the election by [Moro National Liberation Front](2821886.md) (MNLF) leader Nur Misuari[^cite_note-262] due to his background in Mindanao.[^cite_note-263] Other Muslims also supported Duterte and denounced Roxas, the Aquino-supported pick.[^cite_note-264]

On November 6, 2016, Duterte signed an executive order to expand the [Bangsamoro Transition Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangsamoro_Transition_Commission) to 21 members from 15, in which 11 will be decided by the MILF and 10 will be nominated by the government. The commission was formed in December 2013 and is tasked to draft the Bangsamoro Basic Law in accordance with the [Framework Agreement on the Bangsamoro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Framework_Agreement_on_the_Bangsamoro).[^cite_note-265]

Duterte signed the [Bangsamoro Organic Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangsamoro_Organic_Law) on July 26, 2018,[^cite_note-PhilStar-DuterteSignsBOL-266][^cite_note-MB-DuterteSignsBOL-267] which abolished the [Autonomous Region in Muslim Mindanao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_Region_in_Muslim_Mindanao) and provided for the basic structure of government for the [Bangsamoro Autonomous Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangsamoro_Autonomous_Region), following the agreements set forth in the Comprehensive Agreement on the Bangsamoro peace agreement signed between the Government of the Philippines and the Moro Islamic Liberation Front in 2014.[^cite_note-gmafaqs-268]

Duterte signed proclamations granting [amnesty](18940624.md) to members of the [Moro National Liberation Front](2821886.md), and the [Moro Islamic Liberation Front](51673.md) in February 2021.[^cite_note-PNAGovPH-GrantsAmnesty-269]

#### Campaign against terrorism



Duterte welcomes Norwegian Kjartan Sekkingstad following his release from [Abu Sayyaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Sayyaf) captivity.

At the start of 2016, experts warned the Philippines was at risk of having the [Islamic State of Iraq and Syria](9087364.md) (ISIS) set a foothold in the country.[^cite_note-270] However, Duterte's predecessor, President Aquino III, dismissed the perpetrators of a March 2016 armed attack against the military as mere "mercenaries" wanting to join the ISIS.[^cite_note-271][^cite_note-272] The earliest terrorist attacks in Duterte's presidency occurred in [September 2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Davao_City_bombing) in [Davao City](489401.md); [November 2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Butig_clashes) in [Butig, Lanao del Sur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Butig,_Lanao_del_Sur); and in [April to May 2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Bohol_clashes) in [Inabanga, Bohol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inabanga,_Bohol), where the [Abu Sayyaf Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Sayyaf_Group) (ASG) attempted to establish a position in the Visayas.[^cite_note-Laude-2017-273] Though the military eventually quelled the sieges,[^cite_note-Laude-2017-273][^cite_note-274] the events were only a prelude to what would become Duterte's greatest challenge in combating terrorism.[^cite_note-275]

On May 23, 2017, Duterte was in his first state visit to Russia, where he met Russian president [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) to realign the Philippines's foreign policy away from the United States; among his top priorities in the trip included the acquisition of Russian-made munitions to use against Islamic militants in restive Mindanao.[^cite_note-276] At that time, the Philippine military was in an operation to capture ASG leader [Isnilon Hapilon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isnilon_Hapilon). Duterte cut short his trip and flew back to the Philippines after the [Maute Group](50650448.md), which pledged allegiance to the ISIS, [occupied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Marawi) the city of [Marawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marawi) in [Lanao del Sur](297370.md). Upon returning, Duterte [declared Martial law across Mindanao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proclamation_No._216) and ordered a major offensive on the city to retake it.[^cite_note-277][^cite_note-278] The battle, which lasted for five months, became the country's longest urban warfare; the city, particularly the downtown area, was left in ruins,[^cite_note-279] largely due to militant fire and military airstrikes,[^cite_note-CNN-DuterteMarawiLiberated-280][^cite_note-281] necessitating [rehabilitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rehabilitation_of_Marawi).[^cite_note-282] Hapilon and [Omar Maute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omar_Maute) were eventually killed, and on October 17, Duterte declared the city liberated from terrorist influence.[^cite_note-CNN-DuterteMarawiLiberated-280]

Citing the danger posed by Islamic terrorists, Congress, upon Duterte's requests, thrice granted the extension of martial law in Mindanao between 2017 and 2019.[^cite_note-283][^cite_note-284][^cite_note-285] The moves drew criticism, particularly from rights groups, who claimed that the extensions were part of a campaign on stifling dissent.[^cite_note-286] By the end of 2019, martial law in Mindanao lapsed, after which Duterte decided not to have it extended.[^cite_note-287] In July 2020, Duterte signed the [Anti-Terrorism Act of 2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Terrorism_Act_of_2020),[^cite_note-288] which expanded the powers of the government to address the country's growing security challenges. In its enactment, the law repealed the [Human Security Act of 2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Security_Act_of_2007), which was described by the 2020 law's proponents as weak and inadequate.[^cite_note-Santos-2020-289][^cite_note-Mendoza-etal-2021-290]<sup>: 54–55 </sup> However, the 2020 law received intense criticism, notably from the academia and rights groups, for allegedly being prone to abuse; a total of 37 petitions were filed by several groups asking the Supreme Court to have the law nullified,[^cite_note-Mendoza-etal-2021-290]<sup>: 60 </sup> making the law the most assailed legislation in Philippine history;[^cite_note-291] the Court ultimately upheld the validity of the law in December 2021, but struck two of the law's provisions.[^cite_note-292]

As Duterte's term approached its end, the military intensified its efforts against Islamic terrorists, which continued to carry a series of attacks in Mindanao and Sulu;[^cite_note-293] notable among these attacks involved suicide bombing, a tactic not commonly used by Filipino terrorists.[^cite_note-Mendoza-etal-2021-290]<sup>: 55 </sup> In February 2021, Duterte created the National Amnesty Commission, which would process [amnesty](18940624.md) applications of former rebels and determine their eligibility.[^cite_note-294] Additionally, he expanded his administration's reintegration program to include former [violent extremists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violent_extremism).[^cite_note-Gita-Carlos-2020-295] From 2016 to 2021, 1,544 ASG members, 971 [Bangsamoro Islamic Freedom Fighters](39963003.md) members, and 1,427 [Dawlah Islamiyah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dawlah_Islamiyah) members were reportedly captured, killed, or surrendered.[^cite_note-296]

#### Communist insurgency

Duterte described himself as left-leaning during his presidential campaign.[^cite_note-297] As he initially had good relations with the left,[^cite_note-Mogato-2016-298] he received campaign support from leftist groups, particularly in Mindanao.[^cite_note-Gloria-2016-299] Duterte was a student of [Communist Party of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_the_Philippines) (CPP) founder [Jose Maria Sison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jose_Maria_Sison) at [Lyceum of the Philippines University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyceum_of_the_Philippines_University), and Sison stated during the election campaign that Duterte was "the best President the Philippines can have since Marcos".[^cite_note-300]

Upon Duterte's election into office, prospects of restarting peace talks between the Philippine government and the CPP-[NPA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Peoples_Army)-[NDF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Democratic_Front_\(Philippines\)), which stalled in 2011, gained momentum.[^cite_note-Mogato-2016-298] Duterte temporarily released several communist prisoners, notably couples CPP-NPA chairman [Benito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benito_Tiamzon) and CPP-NPA secretary-general [Wilma Tiamzon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilma_Tiamzon), to join the peace talks in Oslo.[^cite_note-301][^cite_note-302] In addition, Duterte offered positions for left-leaning activists in his administration, notably in four executive departments: [Agrarian Reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Agrarian_Reform), [Environment and Natural Resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Environment_and_Natural_Resources), [Social Welfare and Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Social_Welfare_and_Development), and [Labor and Employment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Labor_and_Employment).[^cite_note-Gloria-2016-299] In the months leading to 2017, however, the Duterte administration and the CPP accused each other of not being faithful to the ongoing peace negotiations.[^cite_note-Ranada-2016b-303] The CPP demanded from the administration the release of around 130 political detainees, which—along proposals of the CPP to form a "coalition government"—[^cite_note-304][^cite_note-305] Duterte declined.[^cite_note-Ranada-2016b-303] Additionally, several leftists appointed by Duterte were rejected by the [Commission on Appointments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commission_on_Appointments), while others resigned or were fired by Duterte amidst the tense relations.[^cite_note-CNNPH-DuterteFires-306][^cite_note-Inquirer-LastLeft-307] Compounding these were the continued clashes between the military and the NPA and the alternate declaration and lifting of ceasefire amid the ongoing peace talks;[^cite_note-308][^cite_note-309]



Duterte (2nd from right) presides over a meeting with the [NTF-ELCAC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Task_Force_to_End_Local_Communist_Armed_Conflict) at the [Malacañang Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaca%C3%B1ang_Palace) on April 15, 2019.

On February 6, 2017, Duterte, citing continued attacks by the NPA on the military, formally terminated peace negotiations with the CPP-NPA-NDF, designated them as a [terrorist organization](63578.md#Philippines)[^cite_note-CNNPH-CPP-NPA-310] and ordered the arrest of all NDF negotiators.[^cite_note-311] Through an executive order he issued in December 2018, Duterte established the [National Task Force to End Local Communist Armed Conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Task_Force_to_End_Local_Communist_Armed_Conflict) (NTF-ELCAC), an inter-agency body that involved a [whole-of-nation approach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whole-of-government_approach)—from the national to local level—[^cite_note-312] in addressing the root causes of communism.[^cite_note-313] The NTF-ELCAC worked in close coordination with the military and focused on developing far-flung localities infiltrated by the NPA.[^cite_note-314] Duterte also granted amnesty to former communist rebels[^cite_note-PNAGovPH-GrantsAmnesty-269] and launched reintegration programs to support them and their families.[^cite_note-315][^cite_note-Gita-Carlos-2020-295] Ultimately, in March 2019, he permanently terminated peace negotiations with the CPP-NPA-NDF, facilitating localized peace talks with the rebels.[^cite_note-316][^cite_note-317]

Duterte left office with the number of NPA [guerrilla fronts](12720.md) in the country reduced from 89 to 23,[^cite_note-Nepomuceno-2022-318] and about 20,579 communist rebels reportedly surrendered from 2016 to 2021.[^cite_note-319] Despite its success in reducing communist insurgency in the country,[^cite_note-Nepomuceno-2022-318] Duterte's administration was marred by numerous allegations of [red-tagging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red-tagging_in_the_Philippines), notably the left-leaning [Makabayan Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makabayan_Bloc); Duterte and the military dismissed the red-tagging accusations and argued the government was merely "identifying" them as [communist fronts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_front).[^cite_note-320] Duterte also received criticism after he threatened to order the military in mid-2017 to bomb [Lumad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lumad) schools, which he and the military suspected to shelter rebels and teach subversion.[^cite_note-321] Additionally, the military and the NTF-ELCAC were denounced after they cited clandestine recruitment activities by the communist rebels within university campuses nationwide, notably in both state-run [University of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_the_Philippines) and [Polytechnic University of the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polytechnic_University_of_the_Philippines).[^cite_note-322][^cite_note-323] Several human rights organizations called on the succeeding [Bongbong Marcos administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bongbong_Marcos_administration) to abolish the NTF-ELCAC due to continued red-tagging allegations; the calls, however, were later rejected by Marcos.[^cite_note-324]

#### COVID-19 pandemic

Main articles: [COVID-19 pandemic in the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_the_Philippines) and [Philippine government response to the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_government_response_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic)

Further information: [COVID-19 community quarantines in the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_community_quarantines_in_the_Philippines) and [COVID-19 vaccination in the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_vaccination_in_the_Philippines)

During the early phase of the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md), Duterte downplayed the severity of the outbreak and urged the public to avoid [anti-Chinese sentiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Chinese_sentiment). In a February 2020 press briefing, he assured citizens that "everything is well" and stated that the public should refrain from discriminating against Chinese nationals.[^cite_note-325] The first reported COVID-19 case in the Philippines was on January 30, 2020,[^cite_note-326] prompting Duterte to issue bans on the entry of Chinese nationals from China[^cite_note-327][^cite_note-328] and calling for calm.[^cite_note-329] Duterte changed his tune in March, during which he placed the Philippines under a State of National Calamity[^cite_note-330] and ordered [a lockdown in Luzon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enhanced_community_quarantine_in_Luzon).[^cite_note-331] His administration opted to use what it described as "draconian measures" to control the contagion,[^cite_note-332] and Duterte employed the military and police to enforce the public's strict adherence to quarantine and health protocols.[^cite_note-333][^cite_note-334]

As countries were scrambling in securing COVID-19 vaccines for their own citizens, Duterte called for patience amid criticism.[^cite_note-AP-2021-335] To expedite vaccine procurement, Duterte rejected creating a committee and designated [Carlito Galvez Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlito_Galvez_Jr.) as "vaccine czar", who had the gargantuan task of acquiring vaccines from other countries.[^cite_note-336] Additionally, Duterte personally requested vaccines from world leaders, notably Chinese president [Xi Jinping](2017814.md) and Russian President [Vladimir Putin](32817.md).[^cite_note-337][^cite_note-338] China and Russia responded favorably,[^cite_note-339] and the Duterte administration started [its vaccination program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_vaccination_in_the_Philippines) on March 1, 2021, a day after receiving 600,000 [CoronaVac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CoronaVac) vaccines sent by the Chinese government.[^cite_note-Venzon-2021-340] Duterte also used the [US-Philippines Visiting Forces Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines%E2%80%93United_States_Visiting_Forces_Agreement) as leverage in securing vaccines from the United States, threatening to scrap the military pact.[^cite_note-341] In addition, in an apparent effort to help the Philippines gain access to US-developed vaccines, Duterte granted absolute pardon to US soldier Joseph Scott Pemberton, who was serving prison for [killing transgender Jennifer Laude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_of_Jennifer_Laude) in 2014.[^cite_note-342]



Duterte shows an [AstraZeneca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AstraZeneca)-developed COVID-19 vaccine vial following a ceremonial turnover in [Pasay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasay) City on March 4, 2021.

At the height of the pandemic in mid-2021, the Philippines was a COVID-19 hotspot in Asia; [vaccine hesitancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccine_hesitancy) was a problem, and many citizens opted to wait for Western vaccines to arrive. In exasperation, Duterte urged the public to undergo vaccination, threatening to arrest unvaccinated individuals and order local leaders to compile a list of defiant residents.[^cite_note-AP-2021-335] In May 2021, to encourage the public that vaccines were safe, Duterte—acting on the advice of his doctor—publicly took the [Sinopharm BIBP vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinopharm_BIBP_COVID-19_vaccine) before it was approved for use by the Philippine [Food and Drug Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_and_Drug_Administration_\(Philippines\));[^cite_note-343] his move, however, drew criticism since the general public had access to CoronaVac vaccines, which the public majority perceived to have lower efficacy. The backlash prompted Duterte to apologize, acknowledge the possibility of the Sinopharm vaccine's multiple side effects, and decide to pull out the vaccines.[^cite_note-344] The public's prejudice against China's CoronaVac was later revealed in a [Reuters](18998750.md) 2024 report as the outcome of [a US government-launched covert propaganda and disinformation campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ChinaAngVirus_disinformation_campaign) that aimed to erode the public's trust in China-developed vaccines.[^cite_note-345]

Duterte gradually relaxed quarantine restrictions to revive the economy.[^cite_note-346] Upon leaving office, Duterte's administration secured 245 million vaccine doses; about 70.5 million individuals had been fully vaccinated.[^cite_note-347] Over 3.7 million COVID-19 cases were recorded since the onset of the pandemic, 3.69 million people had recovered from the disease, while 8,706 active cases remained.[^cite_note-348] Though his administration successfully reached its target of vaccinating 70% of the Philippine population,[^cite_note-Inquirer-BoosterSideEffects-349][^cite_note-350] Duterte, along with Health Secretary [Francisco Duque III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Duque_III), was implicated in [a scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharmally_scandal) involving allegedly overpriced medical supplies and equipment. Critics highlighted that Duterte's administration purchased pandemic supplies from Pharmally Pharmaceutical, a company linked to Duterte's former economic adviser, Michael Yang.
